Coherent sheaves, superconnections, and RRG Thanks:
Jean-Michel Bismut, Shu Shen, Zhaoting Wei

Abstract
. Given a compact complex manifold, the purpose of this paper is to construct the Chern character for coherent sheaves with values in Bott-Chern cohomology, and to prove a corresponding Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck formula. Our paper is based on a fundamental construction of Block.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
18G80, 19L10, 35H10

1.
Introduction

Let
X
be a compact complex manifold. Let

K^{\cdot}\left(X\right),K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
be the
Grothendieck groups of the locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-sheaves,
and of the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent sheaves on
X
,
and let
H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
be the
Bott-Chern cohomology of
X
. Set

(1.1)

H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)=\bigoplus_{p=0}^{\dim X}H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{p,p}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right).

There are characteristic classes

K^{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, like the Chern character

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}
, or the Todd class

\mathrm{Td}_{\mathrm{BC}}
, that refine on the classical Chern classes with
values in the even de Rham cohomology
H_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathrm{even}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

In the present paper, we extend the definition of the Chern
character
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K^{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
to a map

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
. In degree
(1,1)
, this map
is just the first Chern class of the corresponding determinant line
bundle of Knudsen-Mumford
[
KnM76
]
. Also we prove a
Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck
formula. Namely, if
Y
is another compact complex manifold, let
f:X\to Y

be a holomorphic map. Let
f_{!}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to K_{\cdot}\left(Y\right)
be the direct image,
and let
f_{*}:H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(Y,\mathbf{R}\right)
denote the
push-forward map.

Theorem 1.1
.

If
\mathscr{F}\in K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
, then

(1.2)

\mathrm{Td}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(TY\right)\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(f_{!}\mathscr{F}\right)=f_{*}\left[\mathrm{Td}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(TX\right)\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(\mathscr{F}\right)\right]\ \mathrm{in}\ H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(Y,\mathbf{R}\right).

When
X
is projective, then

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)=K^{\cdot}\left(X\right)
, and our definition
of
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}
is the standard one. Similarly, when
X,Y
are
projective, Theorem
1.1
is a consequence of
Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck.

We will describe the ideas and techniques that are used to
obtain Theorem
1.1
.

1.1.
The construction of
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}

If
E
is a holomorphic vector bundle on
X
, and if
g^{E}
is a Hermitian metric on

E
, there is an associated unitary Chern connection, whose curvature is of
type
(1,1)
. Using Chern-Weil theory, one can construct
a corresponding Chern character form,

\mathrm{ch}\left(E,g^{E}\right)
, which is closed and lies in

\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
. Results of Bott and Chern

[
BoC65
]
show that the class of
\mathrm{ch}\left(E,g^{E}\right)

in
H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
does not depend on

g^{E}
, which gives us a Chern character map

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K^{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

If
X
is projective, if
E\in K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
, it has a finite
locally free projective
resolution
R
. It can be shown
that
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(R\right)
does not depend on
R
, so that we get a
Chern character map
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
. As shown by Voisin

[
V02
]
, if
X
is not projective,
such projective resolutions may well not exist
1
1

1

In particular,
for a generic torus of dimension
\geq 3
, the ideal sheaf of a point
does not admit a finite locally free resolution.
.

To sidestep this problem, we use a fundamental construction of Block

[
Bl10
]
. To explain this construction, we replace

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
by
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
, the derived category of
bounded
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-complexes with coherent cohomology. In

[
Bl10
]
, Block established an equivalence of categories
between
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
and another category

\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(X\right)
of objects which extend holomorphic
vector bundles. More precisely, an object in

\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(X\right)
is represented by
\mathscr{E}:\left(E,A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
, where
E
a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector bundle which
is a free
\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)
-module, and
A^{E\prime\prime}
is an antiholomorphic superconnection on
E
in the sense
of Quillen
[
Qu85
]
such that
A^{E\prime\prime,2}=0
.

Following earlier work by Qiang
[
Q16
,
Q17
]
, as in the case of holomorphic vector bundles, one can define
generalized metrics on
E
, and use these to
define a Chern character
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, from which we get a map

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, and finally
an associated map
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
. Also we show that in degree

(1,1)
, our Chern character coincides with the first Chern class of
the Knudsen-Mumford determinant bundle
[
KnM76
]
. These results are established by showing
that the analytic constructions are compatible with the underlying
functorial properties of
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
.

1.2.
The Riemann-Roch theorem

Let us now describe the main steps in our proof of Theorem

1.1
. As in Borel-Serre
[
BorS58
, Section 7]
, the proof is reduced to the case where
f

is an embedding or a projection. Indeed, let
i:H\subset X\times Y
be the graph of
f
, and
let
p
be the projection
X\times Y\to Y
. Then
f=pi
. Also
X\sim H
, so that
i
is an embedding
X\to X\times Y
. By functoriality,
we will reduce the proof to the case where
f=i
or
f=p
.

1.2.1.
The case of embeddings

In the case of embeddings, the proof is obtained by a suitable
adaptation of the deformation to the normal cone. The proof relies
mostly on the functorial properties of the above objects
\mathscr{E}
.

1.2.2.
The case of projections

In the case of a projection
p:M=S\times X\to S
, we reduce the proof to the
case where
\mathscr{F}
is represented by an object

\mathscr{E}
in
\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(M\right)
. By a theorem of
Grauert
[
GrR84
, Theorem 10.4.6]
,
p_{*}\mathscr{E}
is known to define an object
in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(S\right)
, but it is not an object in

\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(S\right)
, because it is infinite-dimensional.

Then, we adapt the methods of
[
B13
]
,
where the case of proper holomorphic submersions was considered, when
\mathscr{E}
is a
holomorphic vector bundle, and the direct image

Rp_{*}\mathscr{E}
is locally free, in which case
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(Rp_{*}\mathscr{E}\right)

can be defined without using the theory of Block
[
Bl10
]
. The proof was obtained using the
infinite-dimensional elliptic superconnections on the infinite-dimensional

p_{*}\mathscr{E}
of

[
B86
,
BGS88b
,
BGS88c
]
,
their hypoelliptic deformations
2
2

2

This refers to properties
of the curvatures of the superconnections, that are fiberwise
elliptic differential operators, or fiberwise hypoelliptic operators.
, as well
as local index theory
3
3

3

Local index theory is a method
which computes the local asymptotics of the supertraces of certain
heat kernels as
t\to 0
in terms of characteristic forms.
.

Given metric data
which involve in particular a Hermitian metric on
TX
with Kähler form

\omega^{X}
, we define infinite-dimensional Chern character
forms, and we prove that these forms represent

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(Rp_{*}\mathscr{E}\right)
. For
t>0
, when replacing

\omega^{X}
by
\omega^{X}/t
, if

\overline{\partial}^{X}\partial^{X}\omega^{X}=0
,
we take the limit of our elliptic superconnection forms, and we prove
Theorem
1.1
. When there is no such a metric, we inspire
ourselves from the methods of
[
B13
]
, where
superconnections with hypoelliptic curvature are defined, which
ultimately allow us to prove Theorem
1.1
. In the
constructions and in the proofs, the fact we deal with
antiholomorphic superconnections instead of holomorphic vector
bundles introduces extra
difficulties with respect to
[
B13
]
.

1.2.3.
The spectral truncations

In Subsections
8.10
and
11.1
, we develop
a technique of spectral truncations, which is used when
dealing with the infinite-dimensional
p_{*}\mathscr{E}
. This method
extends classical spectral truncations to families, while preserving critical
cohomological information.

1.3.
Earlier work

Let us describe earlier related work.
In
[
OTT81
]
, O’Brian, Toledo and Tong defined a
Chern character on
K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
with values in the
Hodge cohomology
H^{\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\Omega^{\scriptsize\bullet}_{X}\right)
. In

[
OTT85
]
, they proved a corresponding
Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck formula. Since Bott-Chern cohomology maps
into Hodge cohomology, the above results follow from Theorem

1.1
.

In
[
Le87
]
, Levy
proved a topological version of our result, which is valid for
general complex spaces. Let

K^{0}_{\mathrm{top}}\left(X\right)
be the Grothendieck group generated
by smooth vector bundles on
X
. Levy
constructed a morphism
K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to K^{0}_{\mathrm{top}}\left(X\right)
that extends the obvious morphism

K^{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to K^{0}_{\mathrm{top}}\left(X\right)
, and he
proved the compatibility of this morphism to direct images. Since
Bott-Chern cohomology maps into de Rham cohomology, Levy’s results
are now consequences of Theorem
1.1
.

In
[
Gri10
]
, Grivaux defined a Chern character on

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
with values in the rational Deligne
cohomology ring
A\left(X\right)
, and he proved
Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck for projective morphisms. He also proved a
uniqueness result for characteristic classes on

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
with values in a cohomology theory that
verifies a
simple set of axioms. Recently,
following earlier work by Green
[
Gre80
]
, Toledo-Tong
[
TT86
]
, Hosgood

[
Ho20a
,
Ho20b
]
constructed resolutions of coherent sheaves by simplicially locally free sheaves,
and obtained Chern classes of coherent sheaves in de Rham
cohomology (and in twisted de Rham cohomology), which he showed
to be compatible with the construction of Grivaux

[
Gri10
]
.

In
[
W20
]
, Wu has proved a Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck
theorem for coherent sheaves and projective morphisms. His Chern
character takes values in rational Bott-Chern cohomology. He proves
a Riemann-Roch-Grothendieck for projective morphisms, while our
results are unconditional.

As explained before, the constructions and results of Block
[
Bl10
]
play a
fundamental role in our paper, as an inspiration, and also for
fundamental ideas. Also in
[
Q16
,
Q17
]
, Qiang had
constructed a Chern character
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
by
essentially the same methods as ours. More
detailed comparison with his results will be given in Section

8
.

Moreover, we show that our Chern character

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
verifies
the uniqueness conditions of Grivaux
[
Gri10
]
, and so, it is
compatible with the previous constructions of the Chern
character.

1.4.
The organization of the paper

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we
recall various properties of Bott-Chern cohomology, and we recall the
construction of characteristic classes with values in Bott-Chern
cohomology.

In Section
3
, we state various properties of the
derived category
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
, and of the associated functors.

In Section
4
, we recall elementary properties of
free modules over an exterior algebra.

In Section
5
, we introduce antiholomorphic
superconnections
\mathscr{E}=\left(E,A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
, we
show that the corresponding cohomology sheaf
\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}

is coherent, and that the associated spectral sequence degenerates at

\mathscr{E}_{2}
. In particular, we prove the result of Block

[
Bl10
]
asserting that locally,
\mathscr{E}
is just a complex of holomorphic
vector bundles.

In Section
6
, we establish the result of Block

[
Bl10
]
on the equivalence of categories

\underline{\mathrm{B}}\left(X\right)\simeq\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
.

In Section
7
, given a splitting
E\simeq E_{0}
,
with

E_{0}=\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)\widehat{\otimes}D
4
4

4

In
the paper,
D
is called the diagonal bundle associated with
E
.
If
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
are
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras,

\mathcal{A}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
is the
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra which is
the tensor product of
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
.
, we define the generalized metrics on
E_{0}
, and the adjoint

A^{E_{0}\prime}
of the antiholomorphic superconnection
A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}
. We also introduce the curvature
\left[A^{E_{0}\prime\prime},A^{E_{0}\prime}\right]
. This chapter is the
extension of what is done for holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles,
when constructing the corresponding Chern connection.

In Section
8
, given a generalized metric
h
, we
construct the Chern character form
\mathrm{ch}\left(A^{E_{0}\prime\prime},h\right)\in\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, whose Bott-Chern
cohomology class does not depend on
h
, or on the splitting and
will be denoted
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
. We show
that
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}
induces a map
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, and ultimately a map

K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.
This Section gives another approach to earlier results by Qiang

[
Q16
,
Q17
]
.

In Section
9
, we establish Theorem
1.1
when

f
is an embedding
i:X\to Y
. Using the deformation to the normal
cone, we reduce the proof to the embedding
X\to\mathbf{P}\left(N_{X/Y}\oplus\mathbf{C}\right)
. Also we prove that our
Chern character
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}:K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)\to H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
verifies the uniqueness conditions of
Grivaux
[
Gri10
]
.

In Section
10
, when
f
is the projection
p:M=S\times X\to S
, and
\mathscr{E}
is an antiholomorphic superconnection on

M
, we construct the infinite-dimensional antiholomorphic
superconnection
\left(p_{*}\mathscr{E},A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}\prime\prime}\right)
over
S
. Given a splitting
E\simeq E_{0}
, a metric
g^{TX}
on
TX

with Kähler form
\omega^{X}
, and a Hermitian metric
g^{D}
on

D
, we construct the
L_{2}
-adjoint
A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime}
of
A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime\prime}
,
and we
give a Lichnerowicz formula for the curvature
\left[A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime\prime},A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime}\right]
. Also we construct the elliptic Chern character forms

\mathrm{ch}\left(A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime\prime},\omega^{X},g^{D}\right)
, and
their common Bott-Chern class
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}\prime\prime}\right)\in H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(S,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

In Section
11
, we prove that
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}\prime\prime}\right)=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(Rp_{*}\mathscr{E}\right)
. The proof is
based on the construction of a quasi-isomorphism from a classical
antiholomorphic superconnection
\underline{\mathscr{E}}
to
p_{*}\mathscr{E}
. Spectral truncations play a critical role in the proofs.

In Section
12
, we prove Theorem
1.1
for the projection

p
when
\overline{\partial}^{X}\partial^{X}\omega^{X}=0
. The proof is
obtained by taking the limit as
t\to 0
of the forms

\mathrm{ch}\left(A^{p_{*}\mathscr{E}_{0}\prime\prime},\omega^{X}/t,g^{D}\right)
by
methods of local index theory.

In Section
13
, we construct superconnections with
hypoelliptic curvature. More precisely, if
\widehat{TX}
is another
copy of
TX
, we introduce the total space
\mathcal{M}
of

\widehat{T_{\mathbf{R}}X}
, and also the tautological Koszul complex
associated with the embedding
M\to\mathcal{M}
. Given a splitting

E\simeq E_{0}
, and metrics

g^{TX},g^{\widehat{TX}},g^{D}
on
TX,\widehat{TX},D
, we construct
a nonpositive Hermitian form
\epsilon_{X}
. The adjoint of our new
antiholomorphic superconnection
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{Y}

over
S

is constructed using
\epsilon_{X}
. The corresponding
curvature is a fiberwise hypoelliptic operator. Our constructions
combine the methods of
[
B13
]
with the
results of the previous Sections.

In Section
14
, the hypoelliptic superconnection forms

\mathrm{ch}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{Y},\omega^{X},g^{D},g^{\widehat{TX}}\right)
are constructed, and their Bott-Chern class is shown to be the same as the class of the elliptic superconnection forms.

In Section
15
, when

\overline{\partial}^{X}\partial^{X}\omega^{X}=0
, we give another proof of
Theorem
1.1
for
p
. The results of this Section duplicate
the results of Section
12
, with techniques that prepare
for a proof of the Theorem
1.1
in full generality.

Finally, in Section
16
, we introduce a new deformation of
our hypoelliptic superconnection, in which the
Kähler form is deformed to an object that is still a

(1,1)
-form depending explicitly on the extra coordinate

Y
, and we prove Theorem
1.1
for
p
, which completes the
proof of this Theorem in full generality.

In the whole paper, if
A=A_{+}\oplus A_{-}
is a
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded
algebra, if
a,b\in A
, we denote by

\left[a,b\right]
the
supercommutator of
a,b
, which is bilinear in
a,b
. More
precisely, if
a\in A
, set

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}\,a=

\displaystyle 0\,\mathrm{if}\,a\in A_{+},

\displaystyle 1\,\mathrm{if}\,a\in A_{-}.

If
a,b\in A_{\pm}
, then

(1.4)

\left[a,b\right]=ab-\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}a\mathrm{deg}b}ba.

If
A
is trivially
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded, then
\left[a,b\right]
is the
standard commutator.

2.
Bott-Chern cohomology and characteristic classes

The purpose of this Section is to recall basic facts on
Bott-Chern cohomology of a compact complex manifold
X
.

This Section is organized as follows. In Subsection
2.1
,
we recall the definition of the Bott-Chern
cohomology
H^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.

In Subsection
2.2
, we give various properties of the
Bott-Chern
Laplacian of Kodaira-Spencer, which imply that

H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is
finite-dimensional, that it can be represented by smooth forms or by
currents, and also that it is a bigraded algebra.

In Subsection
2.3
, we describe various functors
that act on Bott-Chern cohomology.

Finally, in Subsection
2.4
, we explain the construction
of characteristic classes of holomorphic vector bundles with values
in Bott-Chern cohomology.

2.1.
The Bott-Chern cohomology

Let
X
be a compact complex manifold of dimension
n
. Let
TX
be
the holomorphic tangent bundle, and let
T_{\mathbf{R}}X
be the
corresponding real tangent bundle. If
T_{\mathbf{C}}X=T_{\mathbf{R}}X\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
, then
T_{\mathbf{C}}X=TX\oplus\overline{TX}
.

Let

\left(\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right),d\right)
be the de Rham
complex of smooth real differential forms on
X
. The de Rham cohomology groups

H\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
are defined by

(2.1)

H^{\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)=\ker d\cap\Omega^{\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)/d\Omega^{\scriptsize\bullet-1}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right).

If
\alpha\in\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
is closed, let

\left[\alpha\right]\in H\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
be its cohomology
class. We may as well replace
\mathbf{R}
by
\mathbf{C}
, and obtain
the complexification of the above objects.

We follow
[
D09
, Section 6.8]
and

[
Sc07
, Section 1]
. For
0\leq p,q\leq n
,
let
\Omega^{p,q}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
be the vector space of smooth
sections of
\Lambda^{p,q}\left(T^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}X\right)=\Lambda^{p}\left(T^{*}X\right)\widehat{\otimes}\Lambda^{q}\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)
. Note that
d
splits as
d=\overline{\partial}+\partial
.

Definition 2.1
.

The Bott-Chern cohomology groups

H^{p,q}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
are given by

(2.2)

H^{p,q}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)=\left(\Omega^{p,q}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)\cap\ker d\right)/\overline{\partial}\partial\Omega^{p-1,q-1}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right).

Then
H^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is a bigraded
vector space.
If
\alpha\in\Omega^{p,q}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is closed, let

\left\{\alpha\right\}
be the class of
\alpha
in

H^{p,q}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.
There is a canonical map

H^{p,q}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)\to H^{p+q}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
that maps

\left\{\alpha\right\}
to
\left[\alpha\right]
.

Then
H^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
inherits from

\Omega^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
the
structure of a bigraded algebra.

Put

(2.3)

\displaystyle\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)=\bigoplus_{0\leq p\leq n}\Omega^{p,p}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right),

\displaystyle H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)=\bigoplus_{0\leq p\leq n}H^{p,p}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right).

The vector spaces

\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right),H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
are preserved by
conjugation. We obtain this way the real vector space

\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
and the corresponding real Bott-Chern
cohomology

H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
. Also

H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
is an algebra.

2.2.
The Bott-Chern Laplacian of Kodaira-Spencer

Let
g^{TX}
be a Hermitian metric on
TX
. We equip

\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
with the associated
L_{2}
Hermitian
product, so that the
\Omega^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
are mutually
orthogonal. If
A
is a differential operator acting on

\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, we denote by
A^{*}
its formal
adjoint. If
A,B
are two operators, let

\left[A,B\right]_{+}=AB+BA
be their anticommutator.

We introduce the Kodaira-Spencer Laplacian
[
KoS60
, Proposition
5]
,
[
Sc07
, Section 2b]
,

(2.4)

\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}=\left[\overline{\partial}\partial,\left(\overline{\partial}\partial\right)^{*}\right]_{+}+\left[\overline{\partial}^{*}\partial,\left(\overline{\partial}^{*}\partial\right)^{*}\right]_{+}+\overline{\partial}^{*}\overline{\partial}+\partial^{*}\partial.

Then
\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}
is a formally self-adjoint
nonnegative real
operator acting on
\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
and
preserving the bidegree. This operator is elliptic of order
4
, with
scalar principal symbol
\left|\xi\right|^{4}_{T_{\mathbf{R}}^{*}X}/4
.

Let

\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
be the vector space of real currents
on
X
, and let

\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
be its complexification. Then
\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)\subset\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.
If
u\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, if

\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}u=0
, since
\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}
is
elliptic, then
u\in\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.

Set

(2.5)

\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)=\ker\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}.

Then
\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)
is also bigraded, and moreover,

(2.6)

\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)=\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial\cap\ker\overline{\partial}^{*}\partial^{*}.

By
[
Sc07
, Theorem 2.2]
, we have the orthogonal splitting

(2.7)

\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)=\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)\oplus\mathrm{im}\overline{\partial}\partial\oplus\left(\mathrm{im}\partial^{*}+\mathrm{im}\overline{\partial}^{*}\right),

and we have the canonical isomorphism of bigraded vector spaces,

(2.8)

H_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)\simeq\mathcal{H}\left(X\right).

In particular, the Bott-Chern groups

H^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
are finite
dimensional.

By the above, if we define Bott-Chern cohomology
using instead
\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, it is
the same as before, and it is still represented canonically by the
smooth forms in

\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)
. While, in general,
the multiplication of currents is not well-defined, the
multiplication of the Bott-Chern classes of currents is still
well-defined via the multiplication of corresponding smooth
representatives.

If
s\in\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
, then

(2.9)

\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}s=\overline{\partial}\partial\left(\overline{\partial}\partial\right)^{*}s,

so that if
s\in\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, then

(2.10)

\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\overline{\partial}\partial s=\overline{\partial}\partial\left(\overline{\partial}\partial\right)^{*}\overline{\partial}\partial s.

In particular
\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}
preserves
\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial

and
\mathrm{im}\overline{\partial}\partial
, while mapping the first vector
space into the second one. By (
2.6
), we have the orthogonal
splitting,

(2.11)

\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial=\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)\oplus\mathrm{im}\overline{\partial}\partial,

\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}
preserves the splitting in
(
2.11
), and
\mathcal{H}\left(X\right)
is its kernel.

For
t>0
, let
P_{t}\left(x,x^{\prime}\right)
denote the smooth kernel
associated with the operator

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)
with respect to the
Riemannian volume
dx^{\prime}
. The operator

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)
preserves
\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
.

For
t>0
, if
s\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, then

(2.12)

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s-s=-\int_{0}^{t}\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\exp\left(-u\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)sdu.

For
t>0
, if
s\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, then

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s\in\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.
If
s\in\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
, by (
2.9
),
(
2.12
), we deduce that

(2.13)

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s-s=-\overline{\partial}\partial\int_{0}^{t}\left(\overline{\partial}\partial\right)^{*}\exp\left(-u\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)sdu.

Therefore if
s\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
lies in

\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
, and if
\left\{s\right\}\in H_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is the
corresponding Bott-Chern class, the form

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s\in\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is a smooth representative
of
\left\{s\right\}
.
Also, as
t\to 0
,
\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s
converges to
s
in

\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.

Proposition 2.2
.

If for
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
s_{n}\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
lies in
\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
, if
s\in\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)

and
s_{n}\to s
in
\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
, then
s\in\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
, and

\left\{s_{n}\right\}\to\left\{s\right\}
in

H_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.

Proof.

It is obvious that
s\in\ker\overline{\partial}\cap\ker\partial
. For
t>0
,

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s_{n}
converges
to
\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)s
in

\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. Also the operator

\exp\left(-t\square^{X}_{\mathrm{BC}}\right)
does not change the
Bott-Chern class. This completes the proof of our proposition.
∎

2.3.
Functorial properties of Bott-Chern cohomology

Let
Y
be a compact complex manifold of dimension
n^{\prime}
, and let
f:X\to Y
be a
holomorphic map. Then
f^{*}
maps
\Omega\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
into

\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
as a morphism of bigraded algebras. Therefore

f^{*}
induces a morphism of bigraded algebras
H_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)\to H_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. Also
f^{*}
preserves the
corresponding real vector spaces.

By duality,
f_{*}
maps
\mathscr{D}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
into

\mathscr{D}^{n^{\prime}-n+\scriptsize\bullet,n^{\prime}-n+\scriptsize\bullet}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
, and it preserves the associated real vector spaces. Since the Bott-Chern cohomology can
be defined using currents, we get a morphism of bigraded vector
spaces
f_{*}:H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)\to H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{n^{\prime}-n+\scriptsize\bullet,n^{\prime}-n+\scriptsize\bullet}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
. This makes
sense, in spite of the fact that, in general,

f^{*}
does not act on
\mathscr{D}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
, and
f_{*}

does not map
\Omega\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
into

\Omega\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
5
5

5

Still, if
f
is a submersion,
both assertions are true.
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be
the total space of
T^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}Y
, so that
Y
is viewed as the zero
section of
\mathcal{Y}
. Let
\mathscr{D}_{f}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
be the set of currents
s
on
Y
such that its
wave-front set
\mathrm{WF}\left(s\right)\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus Y

does not intersect
N_{f}=f_{*}T_{\mathbf{R}}X^{\perp}
6
6

6

This is the set of
the
\left(f\left(x\right),\eta\right)|_{x\in X,\eta\in T^{*}_{\mathbf{R},f\left(x\right)}Y}
such that
f^{*}\left(x\right)\eta=0
.
. By Hörmander

[
H83
, Theorem 8.2.4]
, if
s\in\mathscr{D}_{f}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
is a current on
Y
, the current
f^{*}s
is
well-defined. If
s_{n}|_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\in\Omega\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
is a microlocal
approximation of
s
in
\mathscr{D}_{f}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
7
7

7

This means that there is there is a
closed conic subset
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{Y}
such that

\Gamma\cap N_{f}=\emptyset
,

\mathrm{WF}\left(s\right)\subset\Gamma,\mathrm{WF}\left(s_{n}\right)\subset\Gamma
, and

s_{n}|_{n\in\mathbf{N}}

converges to
s
in
\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma}\left(Y\right)
in the sense
of
[
H83
, Definition 8.2.2]
. By
[
H83
, Theorem
8.2.3]
, given
s
as before, such a sequence

s_{n}|_{n\in\mathbf{N}}

always exists.
, then

f^{*}s_{n}
converges to
f^{*}s
in
\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. If

s\in\mathscr{D}_{f}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(Y,\mathbf{C}\right)
is closed, the above shows that
f^{*}s\in\mathscr{D}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
is closed, and that

\left\{f^{*}s\right\}=f^{*}\left\{s\right\}\,\mathrm{in}\,H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}(X,\mathbf{C})
.

In
[
H83
, Theorem 8.2.10]
, the above argument is used to
show that if
\mathcal{X}
is the total space of
T^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}X
, if

s,s^{\prime}
are two currents in
\mathscr{D}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
such that there
are no
\left(x,\xi\right)\in\mathcal{X}
such that

\left(x,\xi\right)\in\mathrm{WF}\left(s\right),\left(x,-\xi\right)\in\mathrm{WF}\left(s^{\prime}\right)
, the product of currents
ss^{\prime}
is
well-defined and verifies suitable continuity properties. In
particular, if
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
are
closed,
ss^{\prime}
is still closed and moreover,

(2.14)

\left\{ss^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{s\right\}\left\{s^{\prime}\right\}\,\mathrm{in}\,H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right).

2.4.
Bott-Chern characteristic classes of holomorphic vector
bundles

Here we follow
[
BGS88a
, Section (e)]
. Let
\left(F,\nabla^{F\prime\prime}\right)
be a holomorphic
vector bundle of rank
m
on
X
. Let
g^{F}
be a Hermitian
metric on
F
, let
\nabla^{F}=\nabla^{F\prime\prime}+\nabla^{F\prime}
denote the corresponding Chern
connection on
F
. Then
\nabla^{F\prime\prime},\nabla^{F\prime}
act
as odd operators on
\Omega\left(X,F\right)
. Let
R^{F}
be its curvature. Then

R^{F}
is a
(1,1)
-form on
X
with values in
skew-adjoint sections of
\mathrm{End}\left(F\right)
. Then
8
8

8

Recall that
\left[\,\right]
is our notation for the supercommutator.

(2.15)

\displaystyle\nabla^{F\prime\prime,2}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla^{F\prime,2}=0,\qquad

\displaystyle R^{F}=\left[\nabla^{F\prime\prime},\nabla^{F\prime}\right].

By (
2.15
), we obtain the Bianchi identities,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\left[\nabla^{F\prime\prime},R^{F}\right]=0,

\displaystyle\left[\nabla^{F\prime},R^{F}\right]=0.

Let
P\left(B\right)
be an invariant polynomial on

\mathfrak{gl}\left(m,\mathbf{C}\right)
under the adjoint action of

\mathrm{GL}\left(m,\mathbf{C}\right)
.
The polynomial
P
is said to be real if

(2.17)

\overline{P\left(B\right)}=P\left(\overline{B}\right).

Put

(2.18)

P\left(F,g^{F}\right)=P\left(-R^{F}/2i\pi\right).

Then the form
P\left(F,g^{F}\right)
lies in

\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
.
By Chern-Weil theory, the form
P\left(F,g^{F}\right)

is closed, and its cohomology class does not depend on
g^{F}
.
This characteristic class will be denoted

P\left(F\right)\in H^{\mathrm{even}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. If
P
is a real polynomial, then

P\left(F,g^{F}\right)\in\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
, and

P\left(F\right)\in H^{\mathrm{even}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}^{F}
be the space of
metrics on
F
, and let
d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}
be the de Rham operator
on
\mathscr{M}^{F}
. Then
\theta=\left(g^{F}\right)^{-1}d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}g^{F}

is a
1
-form on
\mathscr{M}^{F}
with values in self-adjoint
sections of
\mathrm{End}\left(F\right)
with respect to the given
g^{F}
.

Note that
d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}P\left(F,g^{F}\right)
is a
1
-form on

\mathscr{M}^{F}
with values in
\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. Let

P^{\prime}
be the derivative of
P
. Put

(2.19)

\gamma_{P}\left(F,g^{F}\right)=\left\langle P^{\prime}\left(-\frac{R^{F}}{2i\pi}\right),\theta\right\rangle.

Then
\gamma_{P}\left(F,g^{F}\right)
a
1
-form on
\mathscr{M}^{F}
with values in

\Omega^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
. If
P
is real, this form is also
real.

Let us recall a result of
Bott-Chern
[
BoC65
, Proposition 3.28]
,
[
BGS88a
, Theorem
1.27]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

The following identity holds:

(2.20)

d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}P\left(F,g^{F}\right)=-\frac{\overline{\partial}\partial}{2i\pi}\gamma_{P}\left(F,g^{F}\right).

In particular the Bott-Chern class

\left\{P\left(F,g^{F}\right)\right\}\in H_{\mathrm{BC}}^{(=)}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)
does not depend on
g^{F}
.
If
P
is real, this class lies in

H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

Proof.

Note that
\nabla^{F\prime}
depends on
g^{F}
, and we have the
identity

(2.21)

d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}\nabla^{F\prime}=-\left[\nabla^{F\prime},\theta\right].

Since
R^{F}=\left[\nabla^{F\prime\prime},\nabla^{F\prime}\right]
, we
get

(2.22)

d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}R^{F}=-\left[\nabla^{F\prime\prime},d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}\nabla^{F\prime}\right].

Also

(2.23)

d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}P\left(-R^{F}\right)=-\left\langle P^{\prime}\left(-R^{F}\right),d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}R^{F}\right\rangle.

By (
2.21
)–(
2.23
), we obtain

(2.24)

d^{\mathscr{M}^{F}}P\left(-R^{F}\right)=-\left\langle P^{\prime}\left(-R^{F}\right),\left[\nabla^{F\prime\prime},\left[\nabla^{F\prime},\theta\right]\right]\right\rangle.

Using the Bianchi identities and (
2.24
), we get
(
2.20
). If
P
is real, it is well known the form

P\left(F,g^{F}\right)
is real.

Let us now give another argument taken from
[
BGS88a
, Theorem
1.29]
that shows that

\left\{P\left(F,g^{F}\right)\right\}
does not depend on
g^{F}
. On

\mathbf{P}^{1}
, we consider the canonical meromorphic coordinate

z
and the Poincaré-Lelong equation

(2.25)

\frac{\overline{\partial}^{\mathbf{P}^{1}}\partial^{\mathbf{P}^{1}}}{2i\pi}\log\left(\left|z\right|^{2}\right)=\delta_{0}-\delta_{\infty}.

Let
p_{1},p_{2}
be the projections from
X\times\mathbf{P}^{1}
on

X,\mathbf{P}^{1}
. Let
g^{F^{\prime}}
be another Hermitian metric. Let

g^{p_{1}^{*}F}
be a Hermitian metric on

p_{1}^{*}F
that restricts to
g^{F},g^{F\prime}
on
X\times\left\{0\right\},X\times\left\{\infty\right\}
. By (
2.25
), we get

(2.26)

\frac{\overline{\partial}^{X}\partial^{X}}{2i\pi}p_{1*}\left[p_{2}^{*}\log\left(\left|z\right|^{2}\right)P\left(p_{1}^{*}F,g^{p_{1}^{*}F}\right)\right]=P\left(F,g^{F}\right)-P\left(F,g^{F\prime}\right),

which shows that
\left\{P\left(F,g^{F}\right)\right\}
does not
depend on
g^{F}
. The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Definition 2.4
.

Let

P_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(F\right)\in H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right)

be the Bott-Chern class of
P\left(F,g^{F}\right)
.

If
P
is real, then
P_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(F\right)\in H^{(=)}_{\mathrm{BC}}\left(X,\mathbf{R}\right)
.

Recall that if
B\in\mathfrak{gl}\left(m,\mathbf{C}\right)
, then

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Td}\left(B\right)=\det\left[\frac{B}{1-e^{-B}}\right],

\displaystyle\widehat{A}\left(B\right)=\det\left[\frac{B/2}{\sinh\left(B/2\right)}\right],

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\left(B\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\exp\left(B\right)\right],

\displaystyle c_{1}\left(B\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left[B\right].

The associated characteristic classes are called the Todd class, the

\widehat{A}
class,
the Chern character, and the first Chern class. Also

(2.28)

\mathrm{Td}\left(B\right)=\widehat{A}\left(B\right)e^{c_{1}\left(B\right)/2}.

In Section
12
, we will also use the real form of the
class
\widehat{A}
. More precisely, given
k\in\mathbf{N}
, if
\mathfrak{so}(k)
is the Lie algebra of
\mathrm{SO}\left(k\right)
, if

B\in\mathfrak{so}(k)
, set

(2.29)

\widehat{A}\left(B\right)=\det\left[\frac{B/2}{\sinh\left(B/2\right)}\right]^{1/2}.

To
\widehat{A}\left(B\right)
, one can associate corresponding
Pontryagin forms for real Euclidean vector bundles equipped with a
metric connection.

In the present paper, analogues of equation (
2.20
) will reappear in other contexts,
that include vector bundles of infinite dimension.
9
9

9

The
theory of
Bott-Chern classes
developed in

[
BGS88a
]
is a secondary version of Bott-Chern
cohomology. It is an analogue of Chern-Simons theory for holomorphic
vector bundles. Here, this secondary theory will not play any role.

3.
The derived category
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)

The purpose of this Section is to recall basic properties of the
derived category
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
of
bounded complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with coherent cohomology.

This Section is organized as follows. In Subsection

3.1
, we give the definition of

\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
and of the associated
K
-group
K\left(X\right)
.

In Subsection
3.2
, if
Y
is a compact complex
manifold, and if
f:X\to Y
is a holomorphic map, we define the
derived functor
Lf^{*}:\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(Y\right)\to\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
.

In Subsection
3.3
, we consider the derived
tensor products in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
.

Finally, in Subsection
3.4
, we recall the properties of the
derived function
Rf_{*}:\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)\to\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(Y\right)
.

3.1.
Definition of the derived category
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)

Let
X
be a compact complex manifold. Let

\mathrm{coh}\left(X\right)
be
the abelian category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-coherent sheaves on
X
,
and let
K\left(\mathrm{coh}(X)\right)
denote the corresponding
Grothendieck group. In the sequel, we use the notation
10
10

10

In the introduction, we
used the notation
K_{\cdot}\left(X\right)
.

(3.1)

K\left(X\right)=K\left(\mathrm{coh}\left(X\right)\right).

Let

\mathrm{C^{b}}\left(X\right)
be the
category of bounded complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Let

\mathrm{D^{b}\left(X\right)}
be the corresponding derived category.

Let

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{coh}}^{\mathrm{b}}\left(X\right)
be the full
subcategory of
{\rm C^{b}}(X)
whose objects have coherent cohomology.
Let

\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
be the corresponding derived category. Then
{\rm D^{b}_{coh}}(X)
is the full subcategory of
{\rm D^{b}}(X)
whose objects have coherent cohomology.

The same arguments as in
[
St20
,
Tag
0FCP
]
show that the map

(3.2)

\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)\to\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\mathcal{H}^{i}\mathcal{E}\in K\left(X\right)

induces an isomorphism of
K
-groups,

(3.3)

K({\rm D_{\mathrm{coh}}^{b}}(X))\simeq K\left(X\right).

3.2.
Pull-backs

Let
Y
be another compact complex manifold, and let
f:X\to Y
be a
holomorphic map.

By
[
St20
,
Tag
0095
]
, if
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{coh}}\left(Y\right)
, one can define
its pull-back

f^{*}\mathcal{E}
, which is a complex of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, by the formula

(3.4)

f^{*}\mathcal{E}=f^{-1}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

By Grauert-Remmert
[
GrR84
, Section 1.2.6]
,

f^{*}\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{C}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{coh}}\left(X\right)
. We can define the
left-derived functor

Lf^{*}
, which to
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(Y\right)
associates
Lf^{*}\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a bounded complex of flat
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules, by

[
St20
,
Tag 06YJ
]
, we have

(3.5)

Lf^{*}\mathcal{E}=f^{*}\mathcal{E}.

Also
Lf^{*}
induces a morphism on Grothendieck groups,

(3.6)

f^{!}:K(Y)\to K(X).

If
Z
is another compact complex manifold, if
g:Y\to Z
is a holomorphic map, using

[
Bor87
, Proposition I.9.15]
, there is a canonical isomorphism
between the functors
Lg^{*}Lf^{*}
and
L\left(fg\right)^{*}
. In
particular, we get an identity of morphisms
of Grothendieck groups,

(3.7)

f^{!}g^{!}=(gf)^{!}:K(Z)\to K(X).

3.3.
Tensor products

Let
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
be objects in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
. In

[
St20
,
Tag
064M
]
, a
derived tensor product
\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}
11
11

11

We have replaced
the usual notation
\otimes^{\mathrm{L}}
by
\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathrm{L}}
, to
underline the fact that the objects in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
are

\mathbf{Z}
-graded.
, also an object in

\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
is defined. By

[
St20
,
Tag
079U
]
, if
Y
is a compact complex manifold, and if

f:X\to Y
is holomorphic, if
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
are objects
in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(Y\right)
, then

(3.8)

Lf^{*}\left(\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}^{\mathrm{L}}\mathcal{F}\right)=Lf^{*}\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathrm{L}}Lf^{*}\mathcal{F}.

Let
i:X\to X\times X
be the diagonal embedding, and let

p_{1},p_{2}:X\times X\to X
be the two projections. Since
p_{1}i
,

p_{2}i
are the identity in
X
, using the results of Subsection

3.2
and equation (
3.8
), we find that there is a canonical isomorphism,

(3.9)

\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}\simeq Li^{*}\left(Lp_{1}^{*}\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X\times X}}^{\mathrm{L}}Lp_{2}^{*}\mathcal{F}\right).

By
[
St20
,
Tag
06XY
]
, if
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
are objects in
\mathrm{D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(X\right)
, if
one of them consists of flat modules over
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then

(3.10)

\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{F}.

3.4.
Direct images

Let

Rf_{*}
be the right derived functor of the direct image

f_{*}
. By a theorem of
Grauert
[
GrR84
, Theorem 10.4.6]
, if
\mathcal{E}
is an object in
{\rm D_{\mathrm{coh}}^{b}}(X)
,

Rf_{*}\mathcal{E}
is an object in
{\rm D_{\mathrm{coh}}^{b}}(Y)
.

By
[
Bor87
, Theorem
9.5]
and by
[
D09
, Proposition IV.4.14]
, if
\mathcal{E}
is a bounded complex
of soft
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, then

(3.11)

Rf_{*}\mathcal{E}=f_{*}\mathcal{E}.

Also
Rf_{*}
induces a morphism of Grothendieck groups,

(3.12)

f_{!}:K(X)\to K(Y).

If
Z
is a compact complex manifold, and if
g:Y\to Z
is a holomorphic map, by

[
Bor87
, Proposition I.9.15]
, there is a canonical
isomorphism between the
functors
Rg_{*}Rf_{*}
and
R(gf)_{*}
.
In particular, we have an
identity of morphisms of Grothendieck groups,

(3.13)

g_{!}f_{!}=(gf)_{!}:K(X)\to K(Z).

4.
Preliminaries on linear algebra and differential geometry

The purpose of this Section is to explain elementary facts of linear
algebra and differential geometry that will be used in the rest of the paper.

This Section is organized as follows. In Subsection

4.1
, if
W
is a complex vector space, we introduce
the category
\mathscr{C}_{W}
of free

\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
-modules.

In Subsection
4.2
, we define the
supertraces, and give its main properties.

In Subsection
4.3
, we recall the construction of cones
in homological algebra.

In Subsection
4.4
, we construct generalized Hermitian
metrics on objects in
\mathscr{C}_{W}
.

In Subsection
4.5
, we recall elementary properties of the Clifford
algebras of complex Hermitian vector spaces.

Finally, in Subsection
4.6
, we explain various
relations of the de Rham operator to connections with nonzero torsion on the
tangent bundle of a real manifold.

4.1.
Filtered vector space and exterior algebras

Let
W
be a finite-dimensional complex vector space of dimension

m
, and let
W_{\mathbf{R}}
be the corresponding real vector space. If

W_{\mathbf{C}}=W_{\mathbf{R}}\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
is its complexification,
then
W_{\mathbf{C}}=W\oplus\overline{W}
.

If
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Z},r\leq r^{\prime}
, let

E=\bigoplus_{i=r}^{r^{\prime}}E^{i}
be a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector space on which

\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
acts on the left as linear morphisms
of degree
p
(i.e., they increase the degree by
p
). We can also
define a right action of
\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
on

E
, so that if
e\in E,\alpha\in\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
, then

(4.1)

e\alpha=\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}e\mathrm{deg}\alpha}\alpha e.

For
0\leq p\leq m
, put

(4.2)

F^{p}E=\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)E.

The
F^{p}E
form a decreasing filtration

(4.3)

F^{0}E=E\supset F^{1}E\supset\ldots\supset F^{m+1}E=0.

Put

(4.4)

E_{0}^{p,q}=F^{p}E^{p+q}/F^{p+1}E^{p+q}.

For
0\leq p^{\prime}\leq m
,
\Lambda^{p^{\prime}}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
maps

E_{0}^{p,q}
into

E_{0}^{p+p^{\prime},q}
surjectively.

For
r\leq q\leq r^{\prime}
, set

(4.5)

D^{q}=E_{0}^{0,q}=E^{q}/F^{1}E^{q}.

Then
\Lambda^{p}(\overline{W}^{*})
maps
D^{q}
into

E_{0}^{p,q}
surjectively.

In the sequel, we assume that we have the identification of left

\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
-modules

(4.6)

E_{0}^{\scriptsize\bullet,\scriptsize\bullet}=\Lambda^{\scriptsize\bullet}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}D^{\scriptsize\bullet}.

We will call

D
the diagonal vector space
associated with
E
. There is a canonical degree preserving morphism

E\to D
, and we have the exact sequence

(4.7)

0\to F^{1}E\to E\to D\to 0.

Note that a basis of
D
lifts in
E
to a basis of
E
as a free

\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
-module.

For
r\leq i\leq r^{\prime}
, put

(4.8)

\displaystyle E_{0}^{i}=\bigoplus_{p+q=i}E_{0}^{p,q},

\displaystyle E_{0}=\bigoplus_{i=r}^{r^{\prime}}E^{i}_{0}.

Then
E_{0}
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded
filtered vector
space, which has the same properties as
E
, with the same
D
.

Let
\underline{E}
be another
\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector space like
E
. We underline the
objects associated with
\underline{E}
. If
k\in\mathbf{Z}
, if
\phi
is a linear morphism
from
E
into
\underline{E}
that maps
E^{\scriptsize\bullet}
into

\underline{E}^{\scriptsize\bullet+k}
, we will say that
\phi
is of degree
k
. Let

\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
be the set of
linear maps from
E
into

\underline{E}
such that if

\alpha\in\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
, then

(4.9)

\phi\alpha-\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}\alpha\mathrm{deg}\phi}\alpha\phi=0,

If
\alpha\in\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right),e\in E
, equation
(
4.9
) is equivalent to the fact that

(4.10)

\phi\left(e\alpha\right)=(\phi e)\alpha.

Then
\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded algebra.
There is a left action of
\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
on

\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
by morphisms of degree
p
.

Given
W
, let

\mathscr{C}_{W}
be the category consisting of the above objects
E
that verify
(
4.6
), and of the
morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
.
If
E,\underline{E}
are objects in
\mathscr{C}_{W}
, then

\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
is still an object in
\mathscr{C}_{W}
, and
the
corresponding diagonal vector space is given by

\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\underline{D}\right)
.

If

\underline{E}=\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
, then

\underline{D}=\mathbf{C}
, and

\mathrm{Hom}\left(\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right),E\right)=E
.
Put

(4.11)

E^{{\dagger}}=\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\right).

Then
D^{{\dagger}}=D^{*}
. If
e\in E
, the map
\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\right)\to\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}\phi\mathrm{deg}e}\phi e\in\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
gives the identification

(4.12)

\left(E^{{\dagger}}\right)^{{\dagger}}=E.

Also

(4.13)

E^{{\dagger}}_{0}=\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}D^{*}.

If
W^{\prime}
has the same properties as
W
, by
[
Ke06
, Section
2.3]
, there is a tensor product that is a functor
from
\mathscr{C}_{W}\widehat{\otimes}\mathscr{C}_{W^{\prime}}
to
\mathscr{C}_{W\oplus W^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

If
E,\underline{E}
are objects in
\mathscr{C}_{W}
, the morphism
\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,\underline{E}\right)
is an isomorphism
if and only if
\phi
induces an isomorphism from
D
into

\underline{D}
.

Proof.

We only need to show that if
\phi
maps isomorphically

D
into
\underline{D}
, then it acts isomorphically from
E
into
\underline{E}
.
By (
4.4
), (
4.6
),
\phi
also acts as an
isomorphism from
F^{p}E/F^{p+1}E
into
F^{p}\underline{E}/F^{p+1}\underline{E}
. A recursion argument shows that
\phi
is an
isomorphism from
F^{p}E/F^{p+i}E
into

F^{p}\underline{E}/F^{p+i}\underline{E}
. For
p=0,i=m+1
, we
obtain our proposition.
∎

Observe that
\mathrm{Aut}\left(E\right)
is a Lie group, and
its Lie algebra is just
\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
. There is a surjective group homomorphism
\rho:\mathrm{Aut}\left(E\right)\to\mathrm{Aut}\left(D\right)
, that induces a corresponding morphism of Lie algebras

\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)\to\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
. Put

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aut}_{0}\left(E\right)=\left\{g\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(E\right),\rho g=1\right\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E\right)=\left\{f\in\mathrm{End}\left(E\right),\rho f=0\right\}.

Then
\mathrm{Aut}_{0}\left(E\right)
is a Lie subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}\left(E\right)
with Lie algebra

\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E\right)
.

Let

\mathrm{Aut}^{0}\left(E\right)
be the Lie subgroup of the elements of

\mathrm{Aut}\left(E\right)
of degree
0
. Its Lie algebra
\mathrm{End}^{0}\left(E\right)
consists of
the elements of
\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)
of degree
0
. We use the
notation

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aut}_{0}^{0}\left(E\right)=\mathrm{Aut}_{0}\left(E\right)\cap\mathrm{Aut}^{0}\left(E\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{End}_{0}^{0}\left(E\right)=\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E\right)\cap\mathrm{End}^{0}\left(E\right).

If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{0}\left(E\right)
, then
\left(g-1\right)^{m+1}=1
. Similarly if

f\in\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E\right)
, then
f^{m+1}=0
.

Proposition 4.2
.

If
g\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(E_{0}\right)
, there exists a unique pair

h\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(D\right),f\in\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
such that

(4.16)

g=\exp\left(f\right)h,

and
h=\rho g
.

Proof.

Replacing
g
by
g\left(\rho g\right)^{-1}
, we may as well assume that
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
. Then
g-1

is nilpotent, so that if
f=\log g=\log\left(1+\left(g-1\right)\right)
,
then
f\in\mathrm{End}_{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
. The proof of our proposition is completed.
∎

Observe that

(4.17)

\mathrm{End}\left(E_{0}\right)=\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right).

Any
f\in\mathrm{End}\left(E_{0}\right)
can be written in the form

(4.18)

\displaystyle f=\sum_{0}^{m}f_{i},

\displaystyle f_{i}\in\Lambda^{i}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right).

Then
f\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(E_{0}\right)
if and only if
f_{0}\in\mathrm{Aut}\left(D\right)
.

By (
4.7
), there are non-canonical splittings,

(4.19)

E^{q}=D^{q}\oplus F^{1}E^{q}.

By (
4.6
), (
4.19
), we get the non-canonical
identification of
\mathbf{Z}
-graded
\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)
-modules,

(4.20)

E\simeq E_{0}.

Let
\mathrm{Hom}^{0}\left(D,F^{1}E_{0}\right)
be the homomorphisms of degree

0
of
D
in
F^{1}E_{0}
.
The other
splittings are obtained via the action of
A\in\mathrm{Hom}^{0}\left(D,F^{1}E_{0}\right)
.

If we consider another splitting as in (
4.19
) associated with
A\in\mathrm{Hom}^{0}\left(D,F^{1}E_{0}\right)
, the corresponding
E_{0}
is unchanged, but the
identification to
E
is different. This way, we obtain an
automorphism of
E_{0}
,

g=1+A\in\mathrm{Aut}^{0}_{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
.

4.2.
Supercommutators and supertraces

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{+}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{-}
be a

\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra. If
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, recall that

\left[a,b\right]
is the supercommutator of
a,b
.

If
E
is an object in
\mathscr{C}_{W}
, then

\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)=\mathrm{Hom}\left(E,E\right)
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded algebra,
and so it inherits a corresponding
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-grading. If we fix
splittings as in (
4.20
), then

\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)=\mathrm{End}\left(E_{0}\right)
, and

\mathrm{End}\left(E_{0}\right)
is given by (
4.17
).

Let
\tau=\pm 1
be the involution of
D
that defines its

\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-grading. If
A\in\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, its supertrace

\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[A\right]\in\mathbf{C}
is defined by

(4.21)

\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[A\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\tau A\right].

We extend
\mathrm{Tr_{s}}
to a map from

\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
into

\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, with the convention that if

\alpha\in\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right),A\in\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
,
then

(4.22)

\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[\alpha A\right]=\alpha\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[A\right].

By
[
Qu85
]
,
\mathrm{Tr_{s}}
vanishes on supercommutators in

\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

The linear map
\mathrm{Tr_{s}}
induces a morphism

\Lambda\left(W^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(E\right)\to\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
that does not
depend on the splitting (
4.19
), and vanishes on
supercommutators.

Proof.

As we saw at the end of Subsection
4.1
, the choice
of another splitting is equivalent to the choice of
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{0}^{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
. Such a
g
is necessarily even,
so that if
a\in\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, then

(4.23)

\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[gag^{-1}\right]=\mathrm{Tr_{s}}\left[a\right],

from which our proposition follows.
∎

4.3.
Morphisms and cones

If
\left(Q,d^{Q}\right)
is a bounded complex of complex vector
spaces with differential
d^{Q}
of degree
1
, we denote by

HQ
its cohomology.

Let
\left(Q,d^{Q}\right),\left(\underline{Q},d^{\underline{Q}}\right)
be two such bounded
complexes of
complex vector spaces. Let
\phi:Q\to\underline{Q}
be a
morphism of complexes, so that
\phi
preserves the degree, so
that

(4.24)

\phi d^{Q}=d^{\underline{Q}}\phi.

Then
\phi
induces a morphism
\phi:HQ\to H\underline{Q}
. Also
\phi
is said to be a quasi-isomorphism
if
\phi:HQ\to H\underline{Q}
is an
isomorphism.

Put

(4.25)

C=\mathrm{cone}\left(Q,\underline{Q}\right).

Then
\left(C,d^{C}\right)
is also a bounded complex such that

(4.26)

C^{\scriptsize\bullet}=Q^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}\oplus\underline{Q}^{\scriptsize\bullet}.

The morphism
\phi
can be identified with an endomorphism of
C
, that
maps
C^{\scriptsize\bullet}
into
C^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}
, i.e.,
\phi
can be viewed as a
morphism of degree
1
in
\mathrm{End}\left(C\right)
,
and the differential
d^{C}_{\phi}
is given by

(4.27)

d^{C}_{\phi}=\begin{bmatrix}d^{Q}&0\\
\phi\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}}&d^{\underline{Q}}\end{bmatrix}.

We have the exact sequence of complexes,

(4.28)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underline{Q}^{\scriptsize\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\scriptsize\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

There is a corresponding long exact sequence in cohomology,

(4.29)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.3611pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{\scriptsize\bullet}\underline{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.3611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{\scriptsize\bullet}C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.94579pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.94579pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.74158pt\raise 7.63947pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.88947pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi\left(-1\right)^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 149.69856pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 149.69856pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{\scriptsize\bullet+1}\underline{Q}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

By (
4.29
),
\phi
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if

HC=0
.

For
t\in\mathbf{C}
, let
M_{t}\in\mathrm{End}\left(C\right)
be given in matrix
form by

(4.30)

M_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&t\end{bmatrix}.

For
t\neq 0
,
M_{t}
is invertible, and

(4.31)

d^{C}_{t\phi}=M_{t}d^{C}_{\phi}M_{t}^{-1},

so that
M_{t}
is an isomorphism from
\left(C,d^{C}_{\phi}\right)

into
\left(C,d^{C}_{t\phi}\right)
.

4.4.
Generalized Hermitian metrics

We make the same assumptions as in Subsection
4.1
.
If
e\in W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}
, set

(4.32)

\widetilde{e}=-e.

We still denote by

\widetilde{}
the corresponding anti-automorphism of algebras
of

\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, so that if
\alpha\in\Lambda^{p}\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, then

(4.33)

\widetilde{\alpha}=\left(-1\right)^{p\left(p+1\right)/2}\alpha.

Put

(4.34)

\alpha^{*}=\overline{\widetilde{\alpha}}.

If
A\in\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, let
\widetilde{A}\in\mathrm{End}\left(D^{*}\right)
be its transpose.
Then
\widetilde{}
extends to an anti-automorphism from

\mathrm{End}\left(E_{0}\right)=\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
to
\mathrm{End}\left(E^{{\dagger}}_{0}\right)=\Lambda\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D^{*}\right)
.

If
f\in\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, we define
f^{*}\in\mathrm{End}\left(\overline{D}^{*}\right)
by the formula

(4.35)

f^{*}=\overline{\widetilde{f}}.

Our conventions here coincide with the ones in
[
BL95
, Section 1
(c)]
and
[
B13
, Section 3.5]
.

Recall that

(4.36)

\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)=\Lambda\left(W^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\Lambda\left(\overline{W^{*}}\right),

and that the natural grading
\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)
is given by the sum of the degrees
in
\Lambda\left(W^{*}\right)
and

\Lambda\left(\overline{W^{*}}\right)
. If
\alpha\in\Lambda^{i}\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, we write

(4.37)

\mathrm{deg}\,\alpha=i.

We will often count the degree in

\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right),\Lambda^{q}\left(W^{*}\right)
to be
p,-q
, and we introduce the corresponding degree

\mathrm{deg}_{-}
on
\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
so that if
\alpha\in\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{W}^{*}\right),\beta\in\Lambda^{q}\left(W^{*}\right)
,

(4.38)

\mathrm{deg}_{-}\,\alpha\wedge\beta=p-q.

Most of the time, we will use this second grading on

\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)
. We will still use the notation
\Lambda^{i}\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)

for the forms of total degree equal to
i
.

In the sequel, elements in
D^{i},\overline{D}^{i*}
will be said to
be of degree
i
. If
A\in\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)=D^{*}\otimes\overline{D}^{*}
, we count its
degree as the difference of the degrees in
\overline{D}^{*}
and

D^{*}
. Let
A^{*}\in\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
denote the conjugate of the
transpose of
A
.
We equip
\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
with
the obvious antilinear involution
*
and with the degree induced by
\mathrm{deg}_{-}
on

\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)
and by the above degree on

\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
. Under
*
, this degree is
changed into its negative. An element of

\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
is said to be self-adjoint if it is invariant under
*
.

If
h\in\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
, we can write
h
in the
form

(4.39)

\displaystyle h=\sum_{i=0}^{2m}h_{i},

\displaystyle h_{i}\in\Lambda^{i}\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right).

Definition 4.4
.

An element
h\in\Lambda\left(W_{\mathbf{C}}^{*}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Hom}\left(D,\overline{D}^{*}\right)
is
said to be a generalized metric on
D
if it is of degree
0
,
self-adjoint, and such that
h_{0}
defines a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded Hermitian metric on
the
\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector space
D
12
12

12

This just means that
the
D^{i}
are mutually orthogonal.
. Let
\mathscr{M}^{D}
be the
set of generalized metrics on
D
.

A metric
h\in\mathscr{M}^{D}
is said to be pure if
h=h_{0}
.

If
h\in\mathscr{M}^{D}
, then
h^{-1}\in\mathscr{M}^{\overline{D}^{*}},\overline{h}^{-1}\in\mathscr{M}^{D^{*}}
. If

f\in\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, we define
f^{*}\in\Lambda\left(W^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(\overline{D}^{*}\right)
by the same procedure as
before.

Observe that
\mathrm{Aut}^{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
acts on

\mathscr{M}^{D}
so that if
g\in\mathrm{Aut}^{0}\left(E_{0}\right)
, the action of
g^{-1}
on
\mathscr{M}^{D}

is given by

(4.40)

g^{{\dagger}}h=g^{*}hg.

4.5.
Clifford algebras

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional complex vector space of dimension
n
, and let

V_{\mathbf{R}}
be its real form. Let
V_{\mathbf{C}}=V_{\mathbf{R}}\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}
be the
complexification of
V_{\mathbf{R}}
, so that
V_{\mathbf{C}}=V\oplus\overline{V}
.

Let
g^{V^{*}}
be a Hermitian metric on
V^{*}
, and let

g^{V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}}
be the corresponding scalar product on
V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}
.
Then
g^{V^{*}}
identifies
V^{*}
and

\overline{V}
. Let
c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
be the Clifford algebra of

\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}},g^{V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}}\right)
13
13

13

For convenience,
we prefer to introduce the Clifford algebra of
V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}
instead
of the usual
c\left(V_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
.
, which is generated by
1,f\in V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}
,
and the commutation relations for
f,f^{\prime}\in V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}
,

14
14

14

The commutation relations
are usually written with an extra factor
2
in the right-hand side.
In complex geometry, it is more natural to drop the factor
2
.

(4.41)

ff^{\prime}+f^{\prime}f=-\left\langle f,f^{\prime}\right\rangle_{g^{V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}}}.

As a
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded vector space,

c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
is canonically isomorphic to the exterior
algebra
\Lambda\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
. Let

f^{1},\ldots,f^{2n}
be an orthonormal basis of
V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}
. If

0\leq p\leq n
, and if

1\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{p}\leq n
, the
identification maps
f^{i_{1}}\ldots f^{i_{p}}\in c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
to
f^{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge f^{i_{p}}\in\Lambda\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
. If
B\in\Lambda\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
, we denote by

{}^{c}B
the
corresponding element in
c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
.

If
f\in V^{*}
, if
f_{*}=g^{V^{*}}f\in\overline{V}
, put

(4.42)

\displaystyle c\left(\overline{f}\right)=\overline{f}\wedge,

\displaystyle c\left(f\right)=-i_{f_{*}}.

By (
4.42
),
\Lambda\left(\overline{V}^{*}\right)
is a

c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
-Clifford module. We have the
identification of
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras,

(4.43)

c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}\mathbf{C}=\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda\left(\overline{V}^{*}\right)\right).

In the sequel, we will not distinguish between the two sides of
(
4.43
). In particular, if
B\in\Lambda\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
,
{}^{c}B
is viewed as an element of

\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda\left(\overline{V}^{*}\right)\right)
. In
particular, if
B\in\Lambda\left(\overline{V}^{*}\right)
,
{}^{c}B

is just the exterior product by
B
.

Let
U
be another finite-dimensional complex vector
space. We will view
\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
as an algebra
acting upon itself by left multiplication.

Then

(4.44)

\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\oplus V_{\mathbf{R}}^{*}\right)=\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\Lambda\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right).

Also
\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\oplus V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
can be identified with

\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}c\left(V^{*}_{\mathbf{R}}\right)
. If

B\in\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\oplus V^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)
, let
{}^{\mathrm{c}}B
the
associated element in

\Lambda\left(U^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda\left(\overline{V}^{*}\right)\right)
. If
B\in\Lambda\left(\overline{U}^{*}\oplus\overline{V}^{*}\right)
,
{}^{\mathrm{c}}B
is still multiplication by
B
.

4.6.
A simple remark of differential geometry

Let
Z
be a real manifold, with real tangent bundle
TZ
, and let
\nabla^{TZ}
be a connection on

TZ
, with torsion
T
. Let
\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}
denote
the induced connection on
\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)
.
Let

\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}_{\mathrm{a}}
denote the
antisymmetrization of
\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}
, that acts on

\Omega\left(Z,\mathbf{R}\right)
and increases the degree by
1
.

Let
d^{Z}
be the de Rham operator on
Z
. Then

i_{T}
is a
2
-form on
Z
with values in contraction operators
15
15

15

We will
always use the normal ordering of such operators, so that contraction
operators act before multiplication by forms.
. Let
\left(f_{\alpha}\right)
be a basis of
TZ
, and let

\left(f^{\alpha}\right)
be the corresponding dual basis of
T^{*}Z
. Then

i_{T}
is given by

(4.45)

i_{T}=\frac{1}{2}f^{\alpha}f^{\beta}i_{T\left(f_{\alpha},f_{\beta}\right)}.

We have the classical identity

(4.46)

d^{Z}=\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}_{\mathrm{a}}+i_{T}.

For
a\in\mathbf{R}
, let

\nabla^{TZ,a}
be the connection on
TZ
such that if

U,V\in TZ
,

(4.47)

\nabla^{TZ,a}_{U}V=\nabla^{TZ}_{U}V+aT\left(U,V\right).

Then the torsion of
\nabla^{TZ,a}
is given by
\left(1+2a\right)T
.
Let
\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),a}
be the connection on

\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)
that is induced by
\nabla^{TZ,a}
.

In the sequel, we define the operator
i_{T\left(U,\cdot\right)}

using normal ordering, i.e.,

(4.48)

i_{T}\left(U,\cdot\right)=f^{\alpha}i_{T\left(U,f_{\alpha}\right)}.

By (
4.47
), if
U\in TZ
,

(4.49)

\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),a}_{U}=\nabla_{U}^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}-ai_{T\left(U,\cdot\right)}.

We
have the identity of connections on
\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)
,

(4.50)

\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),a}=\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}-a\left\langle T\left(\cdot,f_{\beta}\right),f^{\gamma}\right\rangle f^{\beta}i_{f_{\gamma}}.

By (
4.45
), (
4.50
), we deduce that

(4.51)

\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),a}_{\mathrm{a}}=\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}_{\mathrm{a}}-2ai_{T}.

By (
4.46
), (
4.51
), we get

(4.52)

d^{Z}=\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),-1/2}_{\mathrm{a}}.

If
U
is a smooth section of
TZ
, then

(4.53)

\left[U,V\right]=\nabla^{TZ}_{U}V-T\left(U,V\right)-\nabla^{TZ}_{V}U.

Equation (
4.53
) can be rewritten in the form

(4.54)

\left[U,V\right]=\nabla^{TZ,-1}_{U}V-\nabla^{TZ}_{V}U.

We define the operator
i_{\nabla^{TZ}U}
using normal ordering, i.e.,

(4.55)

i_{\nabla^{TZ}U}=f^{\alpha}i_{\nabla^{TZ}_{f_{\alpha}}U}.

By (
4.54
), when acting on
\Omega\left(Z,\mathbf{R}\right)
, the Lie derivative operator
L_{U}
is
given by

(4.56)

L_{U}=i_{\nabla^{TZ}U}+\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),-1}_{U}.

Combining (
4.49
) and (
4.56
), we obtain

(4.57)

L_{U}=i_{\nabla^{TZ}U}+\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}_{U}+i_{T\left(U,\cdot\right)}.

Equation (
4.57
) is compatible with equation (
4.46
)
and with Cartan formula
L_{U}=\left[d^{Z},i_{U}\right]
, which can
also be written in the form

(4.58)

L_{U}=\left[\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right)}_{\mathrm{a}}+i_{T},i_{U}\right]=\left[\nabla^{\Lambda\left(T^{*}Z\right),-1/2}_{\mathrm{a}},i_{U}\right].

Let
Z
be a complex manifold, let
TZ
be its holomorphic
tangent bundle, and let
T_{\mathbf{R}}Z
be its real tangent bundle. Let
g^{TZ}
be a Hermitian metric on
TZ
, let

\nabla^{TZ}
be the associated holomorphic Hermitian connection on
TZ
,
and let
\nabla^{T_{\mathbf{R}}Z}
be the induced connection on
T_{\mathbf{R}}Z
, with
torsion
T
. Then

T
splits as
\left(2,0\right)
-form with values in
TZ
and a

\left(0,2\right)
-form with values in
\overline{TZ}
. For
a\in\mathbf{R}
,
\nabla^{T_{\mathbf{R}}Z,a}
preserves
TZ
and
\overline{TZ}
. More
precisely, if
u,v\in TZ
are smooth sections of
TZ
,

(4.59)

\displaystyle\nabla^{TZ,a\prime\prime}=\nabla^{TZ\prime\prime},

\displaystyle\nabla^{TZ,a\prime}_{u}v=\nabla_{u}^{TZ}v+aT\left(u,v\right).

Also equation (
4.52
) splits into separate formulas
for
\overline{\partial}^{Z},\partial^{Z}
.

5.
The antiholomorphic superconnections of Block

The purpose of this Section is to describe the antiholomorphic superconnections introduced by Block
[
Bl10
]
.

This Section is organized as follows. In Subsection
5.1
,
we define the antiholomorphic superconnections
\left(E,A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
on a compact complex manifold
X
.

In Subsection
5.2
, we establish the local conjugation
result of Block, which asserts that locally,
A^{E\prime\prime}

can be reduced to a canonical form. This result is an extension of
the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem for holomorphic vector bundles.

In Subsection
5.3
, when viewing
\mathscr{E}=\left(E,A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
as a sheaf of

\mathcal{O}_{X}
-complexes, we show that its cohomology is a

\mathbf{Z}
-graded coherent sheaf, and that the natural spectral sequence degenerates at
E_{2}
.

In Subsection
5.4
, we show that the complex line
\det D
has a natural holomorphic structure, and that
\det D
coincides
with the
determinant line of Knudsen-Mumford

[
KnM76
]
.

In Subsection
5.5
, we show that
\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}=0
if and only if for any
x\in X
, the complex

\left(D,v_{0}\right)_{x}
is exact.

In Subsection
5.6
, given a morphism of
antiholomorphic superconnections, we construct the corresponding cone.

In Subsection
5.7
, we construct the
pull-backs of antiholomorphic superconnections.

Finally, in Subsection
5.8
, we obtain a tensor
product of antiholomorphic superconnections.

5.1.
The antiholomorphic superconnections

Let
X
be a compact complex manifold of dimension
n
. If
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Z},r\leq r^{\prime}
, let

E=\bigoplus_{i=r}^{r^{\prime}}E^{i}
be a
finite-dimensional complex

\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector
bundle on
X
, which is also a left

\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)
-module, so that elements
of
\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)
act on
E
as morphisms of
degree
p
.

We use the notation of Section
4
, with
W

replaced by
TX
. We assume that the
D^{q}
as defined
(
4.5
) have constant rank, so that the corresponding
diagonal
D
is a complex

\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector bundle on
X
. Also we assume that the analogue of
(
4.6
) holds.

Then
C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right)
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded vector space. The
degree of an operator acting on
C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right)
is
counted as in Section
4
.

Let

P
denote the projection
E\to D
. Then
P

preserves the
\mathbf{Z}
-grading. Also
P
vanishes on
F^{1}E
.

As in (
4.8
), set

(5.1)

\displaystyle E_{0}^{i}=\bigoplus_{p+q=i}\Lambda^{p}\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)\otimes D^{q},

\displaystyle E_{0}=\bigoplus_{i=r}^{r^{\prime}}E_{0}^{i}.

Then
E_{0}
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-graded filtered vector bundle with the
same
D
as
E
.

We can write the analogue of (
4.19
) as a non-canonical
splitting of smooth vector bundles,

(5.2)

E^{q}=D^{q}\oplus F^{1}E^{q}.

As in (
4.20
), we get the smooth non-canonical identification of

\mathbf{Z}
-graded
\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)
-modules,

(5.3)

E\simeq E_{0}.

Any other splitting is obtained from smooth sections of

\mathrm{Hom}^{0}\left(D,F^{1}E_{0}\right)
.

Now we follow Quillen
[
Qu85
, Section 2]
and Block
[
Bl10
, Definition
2.4]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A differential operator

A^{E\prime\prime}
acting on

C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right)
is said to be an antiholomorphic
flat
superconnection
16
16

16

In
[
Bl10
, Sections 2 and 4]
, Block writes instead
that
A^{E\prime\prime}
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-connection.
if it acts as a differential operator of degree

1
on
C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right)
such that

•

If
\alpha\in\Omega^{0,\scriptsize\bullet}\left(X,\mathbf{C}\right),s\in C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right)
, then

(5.4)

A^{E\prime\prime}\left(\alpha s\right)=\left(\overline{\partial}^{X}\alpha\right)s+\left(-1\right)^{\mathrm{deg}\alpha}\alpha A^{E\prime\prime}s.

•

The following identity holds:

(5.5)

A^{E\prime\prime,2}=0.

In the sequel, we will omit ‘flat’, and say instead that
A^{E\prime\prime}
is an
antiholomorphic superconnection.

The operator
A^{E\prime\prime}
preserves the filtration
F
. In particular
A^{E\prime\prime}
acts on
D=E/F^{1}E
like a smooth section

v_{0}\in\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)

which is of degree
1
, and such that
v_{0}^{2}=0
. Then
P
is a morphism of complexes

\left(C^{\infty}\left(X,E\right),A^{E\prime\prime}\right)\to\left(C^{\infty}\left(X,D\right),v_{0}\right)
.

If
g
is a smooth section of
\mathrm{Aut}^{0}\left(E\right)
, then

\underline{A}^{E\prime\prime}=gA^{E\prime\prime}g^{-1}
is another antiholomorphic
superconnection on
E
. In the sequel, we will write that
\underline{A}^{E\prime\prime}\simeq A^{E\prime\prime}
.

Assume for the moment that we fix a non-canonical identification as in
(
5.2
), (
5.3
). Let
A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}
be the
corresponding antiholomorphic superconnection on
E_{0}
. We can write
A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}
in the form

(5.6)

A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}=v_{0}+\nabla^{D\prime\prime}+\sum_{i\geq 2}v_{i},

where
\nabla^{D\prime\prime}
a degree preserving antiholomorphic
connection on
D
17
17

17

In general, its square does not
vanish, i.e., it does not define a holomorphic structure on
D
. The
distinction between antiholomorphic connection and holomorphic
structure will reappear in the sequel.
, and
for
i=0\ \mathrm{or}\ i\geq 2
,
v_{i}\in\Lambda^{i}\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}^{1-i}\left(D\right)
. Since
A^{E\prime\prime,2}=0
, from
(
5.6
), we get

(5.7)

\displaystyle v_{0}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle\left[\nabla^{D\prime\prime},v_{0}\right]=0,\qquad\nabla^{D\prime\prime,2}+\left[v_{0},v_{2}\right]=0.

Put

(5.8)

B=v_{0}+\sum_{i\geq 2}v_{i}.

Then
B
is a section of degree
1
of

\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
, and (
5.6
) can be written in the form,

(5.9)

A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}=\nabla^{D\prime\prime}+B.

Let
g
be a smooth section of
\mathrm{Aut}^{0}_{0}\left(E_{0}\right)

associated with a different splitting in (
5.2
),
(
5.3
). Let
\underline{A}^{E_{0}\prime\prime}
be the superconnection
on
E_{0}
that corresponds to
A^{E\prime\prime}
as before. Then

(5.10)

\underline{A}^{E_{0}\prime\prime}=gA^{E_{0}\prime\prime}g^{-1}.

Then
\underline{A}^{E\prime\prime}
has an expansion similar to
(
5.6
), in which the corresponding terms will also be
underlined. Note that
\underline{v}_{0}=v_{0}
, and also that there
is a smooth section
\alpha
of

\overline{T^{*}X}\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}^{-1}\left(D\right)
such that

(5.11)

\underline{\nabla}^{D\prime\prime}=\nabla^{D\prime\prime}+\left[\alpha,v_{0}\right].

Example 5.2
.

Here are two trivial cases of antiholomorphic superconnections:

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{X}=\left(\Lambda\left(\overline{T^{*}X}\right),\overline{\partial}^{X}\right),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathscr{C}}^{X}=\left(\Lambda\left(T^{*}_{\mathbf{C}}X\right),\overline{\partial}^{X}\right).

In the first case,
D=\mathbf{C}
, in the second case,

D=\Lambda\left(T^{*}X\right)
.
In both cases, we have canonical identifications
E=E_{0}
.

5.2.
A conjugation result

The following extension of the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem has been
established in
[
Bl10
, p.17]
.

Theorem 5.3
.

If
\left(E,A^{E\prime\prime}\right)
is an antiholomorphic superconnection,
if
x\in X
, there is an open neighborhood
U
of
x
, a
holomorphic structure
\underline{\nabla}^{D|_{U}\prime\prime}
on
D|_{U}
such that
\underline{\nabla}^{D|_{U}\prime\prime}v_{0}=0
, and moreover,

(5.13)

(E,A^{E\prime\prime})|_{U}\simeq\left(\Lambda(\overline{T^{*}U})\otimes D|_{U},v_{0}+\underline{\nabla}^{D|_{U}\prime\prime}\right).

Proof.

We will proceed as in proof of the Newlander-Nirenberg
theorem
[
Do88
, Subsection 2.2.2]
. Recall that
\dim X=n
. Let

(z^{1},\ldots,z^{n})
be a coordinate system near
x
such that
x
is represented by
z=0
. For
r>0
small enough, let
U_{r}=\{z\in\mathbf{C}^{n}:|z^{i}|<r\}
be the corresponding polydisc. For
r
small enough, we
can view
U_{2r}
as an open neighborhood of
x
in
X
, such
that

D|_{U_{2r}}
is a trivial smooth vector bundle. In particular,
D
is equipped with the
holomorphic structure
\overline{\partial}
. By (
5.6
), on

U_{2r}
, there is a smooth section
B_{0}
of

\Lambda\left(\overline{\mathbf{C}}^{n}\right)\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{End}\left(D\right)
of
degree
1
such that

(5.14)

A^{E_{0}\prime\prime}|_{U_{2r}}=\overline{\partial}+B_{0}.

If
B_{0}^{(\geq 1)}
is the sum of the components of
B_{0}
of degree
\geq 1
in

\Lambda\left(\overline{\mathbf{C}}^{n}\right)
, then

(5.15)

B_{0}=v_{0}+B_{0}^{(\geq 1)}.

For
t\in\mathbf{C},\left|t\right|\leq 1
, consider the
